Figure 10
.
Rules of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}
.

A formula
A
of
\mathsf{iCPL}
is
purely classical
if
A
contains no intuitionistic propositional variable, and

purely intuitionistic
if
A
contains no classical propositional variable.

Let us list a few properties of
\mathsf{iCPL}
.

Lemma A.1.

For any purely Boolean formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
,
\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
is derivable in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
is a tautology.

Lemma A.2.

The following are theorems of
\mathsf{iCPL}
:

(i.)

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}\leftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
and
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}\leftrightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}
;

(ii.)

if
a\notin\mathsf{FN}(A)
,
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}(A\to B)\to(A\to\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B)
.

(iii.)

if
a\notin\mathsf{FN}(A)
,
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}(A\land B)\leftrightarrow A\land\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
.

iv.

if
a\notin\mathsf{FN}(A)
, then
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\leftrightarrow A
.

Lemma A.3.

For any Boolean formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
with
\mathsf{FN}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})\subseteq\{a\}
,
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\lor(\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})
is provable.

Proof.

Two possibilities arise:
if
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})\geq q
, then from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
we deduce, by (
\mathbf{C}
I),
\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
as well as

\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\lor(\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})
.
if
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})>q
, then we deduce, using axiom

C
⊥

,

\vdash\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
, as well as

\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\lor(\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})
.
∎

A.2.
Relating
\mathsf{iCPL}
and
\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}
.

Let us first provide a complete proof of the decomposition lemma (Lemma
3.3
).

For any formula
C
and
b\in\{0,1\}
, let
\lnot^{b}C
indicate the formula
C
if
b=0
and
\lnot C
if
b=1
.
For any
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
, let the
theory of
\omega
be the following set of Boolean formulas:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)=\{\lnot^{(1-\omega(a)(i))}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\mid a,i\in\mathbb{N}\}

Moreover, for all
K\in\mathbb{N}
and finite set
X
, let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{K}_{X}(\omega)
be the Boolean formula below

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{K}_{X}(\omega)=\bigwedge_{a\in A,i\leq K}\lnot^{(1-\omega(a)(i))}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}

Observe that the formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{K}_{X}(\omega)
only depends on a finite amount of information of
\omega
, namely, on the unique
v\in(2^{\{1,\dots,K\}})^{X}
such that
v(a)(i)=\omega(a)(i)
.
When
v
is clear from the context, we will indicate
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{K}_{X}(\omega)
simply as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)
.

The decomposition lemma can be reformulated then as follows:

Lemma A.4 (decomposition lemma, syntactic formulation).

For any formula
A
of
\mathsf{iCPL}
there exist intuitionistic formulas
A_{v}
, where
v
varies over all possible valuations of the Boolean variables in
A
,
such that

\vdash A\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{v}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land A_{v}
.

Proof.

The formula
A_{v}
is defined by induction on
A
as follows:

\bullet

if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}
,
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
, or
A={p}
, then
A_{v}=A
;

\bullet

if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}
, then
A_{v}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}
if
v(a)(i)=1
, and
A_{v}={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
if
v(a)(i)=0
;

\bullet

if
A=BcC
, where
c=\land,\lor,\to
, then
A_{v}=B_{v}cC_{v}
;

\bullet

if
A=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
, then we consider two cases:

\bullet

if
a
does not occur in
B
, then we let
A_{v}:=B_{v}
;

\bullet

if
a
does occur in
B
, then for all valuation
w
of the variables of name
a
in
B
, we can suppose that the formulas
B_{v+w}
are well-defined. We let then

A_{v}=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}\bigvee_{w}B_{v+w}
.

Let us show that for any formula
A
and valuation
v
of its Boolean variables,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\vdash_{\mathsf{iCPL}}A\leftrightarrow A_{v}
.
We argue by induction on
A
. Indeed, the only non-trivial case is that of
A=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
.
If
a
does not occur in
B
, then we can conclude by the IH.
Otherwise, by the IH we have that for any valuation
w
of the Boolean variables of
B
of name
a
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v+w)\vdash B\leftrightarrow B_{v+w}
. Using the fact that
\bigvee_{w}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w)
is provable and that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v+w)\leftrightarrow({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w))
, we deduce then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\vdash B\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{w}B_{v+w}
, from which we can deduce

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\vdash A\leftrightarrow A_{v}
.

Using
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\vdash A\leftrightarrow A_{v}
and
\vdash\bigvee_{v}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)
, we can conclude

\vdash A\leftrightarrow\bigvee_{v}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land A_{v}
.

∎

If
A
is a formula of
\mathsf{iCPL}
containing no Boolean variable, we let
|A|
indicate the corresponding formula of
\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}
obtained by deleting names from counting quantifiers, i.e. replacing
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}
by
\mathbf{C}^{q}
.

In the following, we consider as
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
the proof-system obtained by enriching the rules from Section
3
with all standard rules for intuitionistic connectives (straightforwardly adapted to the sequents of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
by adding everywhere a fixed Boolean formula
\dots\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\dots
).

Lemma A.5.

In
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
, if
\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}\rightarrowtail A
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}
is satisfiable, then

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail A
.

Proof.

By induction on the rules of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
.

∎

The following result describes the correspondence between the proof-theories of
\mathsf{iCPL}
and
\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}
.

Proposition A.6.

\Gamma\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}}A
iff for all valuations
v
,
|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail|A_{v}|
.

Proof.

The if part follows from the decomposition lemma (Lemma
3.3
) and from the fact that

|\Gamma|\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail|A|
implies
\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}}A
(easily checked by induction on the rules of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
).

For the only-if part we argue by induction on the rules of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}
. All propositional cases are straightforward, so we only focus here on the rules for counting quantifiers.

(
\mathbf{C}
I):

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.75pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\vdash A$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to98.23955pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 27.22876pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\mapsto\qquad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 36.2576pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\left\{\hbox{\ignorespaces\minipage{345.0pt}$\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 11.25186pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 8.79224pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w)\rightarrowtail|A_{v+w}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-1.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-1.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to157.52786pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\hss}\raise 2.0pt\hbox to157.52786pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}}\lower 0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt($\vee$I)}}\vskip-1.49812pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w)\rightarrowtail\bigvee_{w}|A_{v+w}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces$
\endminipage
}\right\}_{w\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip-1.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-1.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to381.92798pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\hss}\raise 2.0pt\hbox to381.92798pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}}\lower 0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt($\mathsf{m}$)}}\vskip-1.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 124.57564pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\rightarrowtail\bigvee_{w}|A_{v+w}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-2.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 124.57564pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to335.69264pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt($\mathbf{C}$I)}}\vskip-2.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 236.07951pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail|(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A)_{v}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces

(
\mathbf{C}
E)
1
:

Since
a\notin\mathsf{FN}(A)
, it follows that
(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A)_{v}=A_{v}
, so we can conclude by the IH.

(
\mathbf{C}
E)
2
:

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 14.47221pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,A\vdash B$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to96.68355pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 24.48087pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{qs}_{a}B$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\mapsto\qquad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 45.75948pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail|(\mathbf{C}_{a}^{q}A)_{v}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\left\{\hbox{\ignorespaces\minipage{345.0pt}$\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 20.75374pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|,|A_{v+w}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w)\rightarrowtail|B_{v+w}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip-2.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to175.44923pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt[Lemma \ref{lemma:nothv}]}}\vskip-2.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 18.03357pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|,|A_{v+w}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail|B_{v+w}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-1.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 5.35243pt\lower-1.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to164.74435pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\hss}\raise 2.0pt\hbox to164.74435pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}}\lower 0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt($\vee$I)}}\vskip-1.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 5.35243pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|,|A_{v+w}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail(\bigvee_{w}|B_{v+w}|)$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces$
\endminipage
}\right\}_{w}$}\hskip 4.0pt}\vskip-1.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-1.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to372.80676pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\hss}\raise 2.0pt\hbox to372.80676pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}}\lower 0.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt($\vee$E)}}\vskip-1.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 95.79451pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|,\bigvee_{w}|A_{v+w}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail(\bigvee_{w}|B_{v+w}|)$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\vskip-2.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to404.1509pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt($\mathbf{C}$E)}}\vskip-2.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 143.7631pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail|(\mathbf{C}^{qs}_{a}B)_{v}|$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces

∎

A.3.
Normalization in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}
.

It is possible to define a normalization procedure for
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}
. Cuts formed by propositional intro-elim rules are reduced as in standard intuitionistic logic. The cuts formed by counting rules are reduced as follows:

(
\mathbf{C}
I/
\mathbf{C}
E
1
):

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 27.4204pt\hbox{\vbox{\vbox{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 11.56075pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Pi$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\vdash A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}\hbox{\hskip 14.45377pt}\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-2.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to98.23955pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$(\mathbf{C}$I)}}\vskip-2.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 27.22876pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-2.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 27.22876pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to43.78201pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt($\mathbf{C}$E)${}_{1}$}}\vskip-2.49812pt\hbox{\hskip 32.41148pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}}\ignorespaces\qquad\leadsto\qquad\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 8.44293pt\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\hskip 6.65967pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Pi^{*}$}\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma\vdash A$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\ignorespaces

where the derivation
\Pi^{*}
is obtained from Lemma
A.7
below, easily established by induction.

Lemma A.7.

If
\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\vdash A
is derivable in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}
, where
\mathsf{FN}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})\subseteq\{a\}
, and
a
does not occur in either
\Gamma
nor
A
, then
\Gamma\vdash A
is derivable with a derivation of same length and using the same rules.

(
\mathbf{C}
I/
\mathbf{C}
E
2
):

\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\lower 2.0pt\hbox{\hskip 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}\hbox{\vbox{\vskip 1.0pt}}\lower 0.0pt\hbox{}}\vskip 2.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma,A\vdash B$}\hskip 4.0pt}}}}\vskip-2.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 4.0pt}}\vskip-2.49814pt\hbox{\hskip 29.90738pt\lower-0.50186pt\hbox{}\hbox to167.08795pt{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}}\lower 1.99812pt\hbox{\hskip 3.0pt$(\mathbf{C}$I)}}\vskip-2.49812pt\hbox{\hskip where the admissibility of the rule (subst) is easily checked by induction.

The proof of
Theorem
A.6
yields a way to associate each proof
\Pi
of
\Gamma\vdash A
in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}
with a family of proofs
\Pi_{v}
of
|\Gamma_{v}|\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\rightarrowtail|A_{v}|
in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}^{-}
.
This association preserves normalization in the following sense:

Lemma A.8.

For any two proofs
\Pi,\Sigma
of
\Gamma\vdash A
in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}
and any valuation
v
of the Boolean variables in
\Gamma
and
A
, if
\Pi\leadsto^{*}\Sigma
, then
\Pi_{v}\leadsto^{*}\Sigma_{v}
.

The lemma above, easily checked, can be used to deduce the strong normalization of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}
from that of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
:

Theorem A.9.

\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}^{-}
is strongly normalizing.

A.4.
Soundness and Completeness of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}
.

Let us first establish a few properties of Kripke Semantics.
For this we first need to recall a fundamental property of Borel sets.

For any Borel set
S\in\mathbf{B}_{X\cup Y}
and
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}
, let

\Pi^{\omega}(S)=\{\omega^{\prime}\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{Y}\mid\omega+\omega^{\prime}\in S\}\subseteq(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}

Notice that
\Pi^{\omega}(S)
is an
analytic
set and needs not be Borel. However, since the Lebesgue measure is defined on all analytic sets, the values
\mu(\Pi^{\omega}(S))
, for
S
Borel (or more generally, analytic), are always defined. Moreover the following holds:

Lemma A.10.

[
[
Kechris
]
, Theorem 14.11 + Theorem 29.26]
For any
S\in\mathbf{B}_{X\cup Y}
, with
X\cap Y=\emptyset
, and
r\in[0,1]
,

\{\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}\mid\mu(\Pi^{f}(S))\geq r\}\in\mathbf{B}_{X}
.

For any
S\subseteq\mathbf{B}_{X}
and
Y\supseteq X
, let
S^{\uparrow Y}:=S\times(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{Y-X}
.

Using Lemma
A.10
we can show that for any
\mathsf{iCPL}
-structure
{M}=(W,\preceq,\mathbf{i})
and world
w\in W
, the set of functions
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}
such that
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
is a Borel set.

Lemma A.11.

Given a
\mathsf{iCPL}
-structure
{M}=(W,\preceq,\mathbf{i})
, for any finite set
X
,
w\in W
and formula
A
with
\mathrm{FN}(A)\subseteq X
, the set

\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)=\{\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}\mid w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A\}

is Borel.

Proof.

We argue by induction on
A
:

\bullet

if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
, then
\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)=\emptyset
is Borel;
similarly, if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}
, then
\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)=(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}
is Borel;

\bullet

if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}
, then
\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)
is the cylinder
\{f\mid f(a)(i)=1\}
, so it is Borel;

\bullet

if
A={p}
, then
\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)
is either
(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}
or the empty set, which are both Borel;

\bullet

if
A=B\land C
, then
\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)=\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(B,X,w)\cap\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(C,X,w)
, so we conclude by the I.H.;

\bullet

if
A=B\lor C
, then
\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)=\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(B,X,w)\cup\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(C,X,w)
, so we conclude by the I.H.;

\bullet

if
A=B\to C
, then
\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)=\bigcap_{w^{\prime}\succeq w}\overline{\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(B,X,w^{\prime})}\cup\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(C,X,w^{\prime})
is, by the I.H., a countable intersection of Borel sets, so it is Borel;

\bullet

if
A=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
, then by the I.H. , for all
w^{\prime}\in W
,

\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(B,X\cup\{a\},w^{\prime})
is Borel; using Lemma
A.10
we then have that the set

\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(A,X,w)=\bigcap_{w^{\prime}\succeq w}\left\{\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}\mid\mu\left(\Pi^{\omega}\left(\mathrm{mod}_{{M}}(B,X\cup\{a\},w^{\prime})\right)\right)\geq q\right\}

is Borel.

∎

Lemma A.12 (monotonicity).

If
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
and
w\preceq w^{\prime}
, then
w^{\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
.

Proof.

We argue by induction on
A
.

If
A={p}
the claim follows from the fact that
\mathbf{i}({p})
is an upper set;

If
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}
the claim is immediate.

If
A=B\land C
, the claim follows from the I.H.

If
A=B\to C
and

w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
, then for all
w^{\prime\prime}\succeq w
,

w^{\prime\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}B
implies
w^{\prime\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}C
. Hence, in particular, for all
w^{\prime\prime}\succeq w^{\prime}\succeq w
,
w^{\prime\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}B
implies
w^{\prime\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}C
, and thus

w^{\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
.

If
A=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
then by the I.H. for all
\omega^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
,
w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}B
implies that for all
w^{\prime}\succeq w
,
w^{\prime},\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}B
; we deduce then that for for all
w^{\prime}\succeq w
,
for all
w^{\prime\prime}\succeq w^{\prime}\succeq w
, the set
\left\{\omega^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}\mid w^{\prime\prime},\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}B\right\}
has measure
\geq q
, which implies
w^{\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
.
∎

Lemma A.13.

Let
{M}=\langle W,\preceq,\alpha\rangle
be a
\mathsf{iCPL}
-structure.
For any finite set
X
and
a\notin X
, for all

u\in W
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\in\mathbf{B}_{X}
, and for any formula
A\in\mathrm{Formulae}_{X}
,
if
u,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
holds, then
u,\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A
holds for any
\omega^{\prime}\in\{\omega\}^{\uparrow_{X\cup\{a\}}}
.

Proof.

By induction on the rules.
∎

Lemma A.14.

Let
{M}=\langle W,\preceq,\mathbf{i}\rangle
be a
\mathsf{iCPL}
-structure.
For any finite set
X
,
a\notin X
, formula
A\in\mathrm{Formulae}_{X}
,
q\in(0,1]
and
\omega\in\mathbf{B}_{X}
,

w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\quad\Rightarrow\quad w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A

Proof.

w^{\prime},\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
implies that

\mu(\{\omega^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}\mid u,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A\})\geq q>0
. From
u,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A
one can deduce by induction on
A
that
u,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
. Since there exists at least one such
f
, we conclude that
u,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
holds.

∎

We are now in a position to establish, by induction on the rules, the soundness of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}
.

Proposition A.15 (soundness).

If
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}}A
, then
\Gamma\vDash A
.

Proof.

Let
{M}=\langle W,\preceq,\mathbf{i}\rangle
be a
\mathsf{iCPL}
-structure.
We argue by induction on the rules of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}
(selected rules):

\bullet

if the last rule is

\Gamma,A\vdash A

, then for all
w\in W
and
\omega\in\mathbf{B}_{X}
, if
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\Gamma,A
, then

w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
.

\bullet

if the last rule is

\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}A

\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q

\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

then by the I.H. together with Lemma
A.12
, for all
w\in W
, for all
\omega\in\mathbf{B}_{X}
and
\omega^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
, if
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\Gamma
and
w,\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{\{a\}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}
(where the latter condition only depends on
\omega^{\prime}
), then for all
w^{\prime}\succeq w
, we have

w^{\prime},\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A
.

Since
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q
, we deduce that for all
w^{\prime}\succeq w
,

\left\{\omega^{\prime}\mid w^{\prime},\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A\right\}

has measure greater than
q
, and we conclude then that
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
.

\bullet

if the last rule is

\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

\Gamma,A\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}C

(
\mathbf{C}
E
1
)
a\notin\mathrm{FN}(\Gamma,C)

\Gamma\vdash C

let
w\in W
,
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}
and
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\Gamma
.
By I.H. we deduce that the set

S:=\left\{\omega^{\prime}\mid w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A\right\}

has measure greater than
q>0
, and it is thus non-empty.
Let
\omega^{\prime}
be an element of
S
; again, by the I.H. we deduce that
w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}C
;
since
a\notin\mathsf{FN}(C)
, by Lemma
A.14
, we conclude then that
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}C
.

\bullet

if the last rule is

\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A

\Gamma,A\vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}C

(
\mathbf{C}
E
2
)
a\notin\mathrm{FN}(\Gamma)

\Gamma\vdash\mathbf{C}^{qs}_{a}C

let
w\in W
,
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}
and
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\Gamma
.
By I.H. we deduce that the set

S:=\left\{\omega^{\prime}\mid w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A\right\}

has measure greater than
q\geq qs
and is contained in the set

S^{\prime}:=\left\{\omega^{\prime}\mid w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}C\right\}
.
We can conclude then that
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\mathbf{C}^{qs}_{a}C
.

\bullet

Axiom (

C
∨

) is valid: suppose
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}(A\lor B)
, where
a\notin\mathsf{FN}(A)
. Then the set
\{\omega^{\prime}\mid w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A\lor B\}
has measure greater than
q
.
Observe that
w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A
holds iff
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
; so suppose
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
does not hold; then for any
\omega^{\prime}
,

w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}A
does not hold, and thus the set

\{\omega^{\prime}\mid w,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{M}}B\}
must have measure greater than
q
.
We have thus proved that either
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}A
holds
or
w,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{M}}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
holds, and thus that

w,\omega\Vdash^{X}A\lor\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
.

\bullet

Axiom (

C
⊥

) is valid:
suppose
\mathsf{FN}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})\subseteq\{a\}
and
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})<q
; for any
w\in W
, the set
\{\omega^{\prime}\mid w,\omega^{\prime}\vdash^{\{a\}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\}
coincides with
\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rrbracket
, and has thus measure
<q
by hypothesis; we deduce that for any
w^{\prime}\geq w
,
w^{\prime},*\Vdash^{\emptyset}_{{M}}\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
does not hold, and we conclude that
w,*\Vdash^{\emptyset}_{{M}}\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}
.

∎

To prove the completeness theorem we first need to study provability in
\mathsf{iCPL}
a bit further.

Definition A.16.

For any formula
A
and finite set
X
,
|A|_{X}:=\max\{i\mid\exists a\in X\cap\mathsf{FN}(A)\text{ s.t. }{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\text{ occurs in }A\}
. We use
|A|
as a shorthand for
|A|_{\mathsf{FN}(A)}
.
For any
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
,

A^{\omega}:={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{|A|}_{\mathsf{FN}(A)}(\omega)\to A
.

We will exploit the following relativized version of the decomposition lemma (Lemma
3.3
), which is proved in a similar way:

Lemma A.17.

Let
A
be a formula with
\mathsf{FN}(A)\subseteq X\cup Y
, where
X\cap Y=\emptyset
. Then there exist formulas
A^{X}_{w}
, with
\mathsf{FN}(A^{X}_{w})\subseteq X
, where
w
ranges over the valuations of the variables of name in
Y
in
A
,
such that

\vdash A\leftrightarrow(\bigvee_{w}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w)\land A^{X}_{w})
.

Definition A.18.

For each formula
A
, with
\mathsf{FN}(A)\subseteq\{a\}
, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}:=\bigvee_{v}\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\mid A_{v}\not\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}\}
.

From
A\vdash\bigvee_{v}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land A_{v}
and
A\vdash\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w)
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(w)
not occurring in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}
, we can deduce
A\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}
.
The following result shows that measuring the formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}
provides a test to know if
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
is consistent:

Corollary A.19.

Let
A
be a formula with
\mathsf{FN}(A)\subseteq\{a\}
. Then for all
q\in(0,1]
,

\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A})<q\quad\Rightarrow\quad\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}

Proof.

The claim follows from the observation that
A\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}
by applying Axiom (

C
⊥

) and the rule (
\mathbf{C}
E
2
).∎

Remark 2 (relativizing
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}
to
\omega
).

Suppose
A
is a formula with
\mathsf{FN}(A)\subseteq X\cup\{a\}
(with
a\notin X
). Then for each
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
, let
A^{X}_{\omega}
be
A^{X}_{w}
, where
w
is the restriction of
\omega
to
(2^{|A|})^{X}
.
Then, by applying the construction from Lemma
A.17
to
A^{X}_{\omega}
we can define a Boolean formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}^{\omega}
with
\mathsf{FN}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}^{\omega})\subseteq\{a\}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{|A|}_{X}(\omega)\vdash A\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}^{\omega}
and such that

\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{A}^{\omega})<q\Rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{|A|}_{X}(\omega)\vdash\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A
.

The proof of the completeness theorem relies on the construction of a suitable “canonical model” based on sets of formulas. Let us first introduce some terminology.
Let
\Gamma
be a (possibly infinite) set of formulas. We say that
\Gamma
is

\bullet

A
-consistent
if
\Gamma\not\vdash A
;

\bullet

consistent
if it is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
-consistent;

\bullet

super-consistent
if for all
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma
is consistent;

\bullet

closed
if
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\in\Gamma
and
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}}A
implies
A\in\Gamma
, and if
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\to A)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
and
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q
then
(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
.

Let
\Theta
be the set of all super-consistent and closed sets of formulas
\Gamma
satisfying the following conditions, for all
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
:

(
\vee
-closure)

\displaystyle(A\lor B)^{\omega}\in\Gamma\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad A^{\omega}\in\Gamma\text{ or }B^{\omega}\in\Gamma

(
\mathbf{C}
-closure)

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A)^{\omega}\in\Gamma\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\text{ s.t. }\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q\text{ and }({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\to A)^{\omega}\in\Gamma

The fundamental ingredient for the completeness theorem is the lemma below, which will be used to lift any consistent set of formulas to an element of
\Theta
.

Lemma A.20 (saturation lemma).

Let
\Gamma
be a super-consistent set and let
\omega_{0}\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
be such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})\cup\Gamma
is
A
-consistent.
Then there exists a set
\Gamma^{*}\supseteq\Gamma
such that
\Gamma^{*}\in\Theta
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}_{X}(\omega_{0})\cup\Gamma^{*}
is
A
-consistent.

Proof.

Let us fix an enumeration
(a_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of all names.
For any
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
, Let
{F}_{p,q}
be the set of all formulas
B
such that
\mathsf{FN}(B)\subseteq\{a_{0},\dots,a_{q-1}\}
and
|B|\leq p
. Let us fix, for all
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
an enumeration
(C_{n}^{p,q})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
{F}_{p,q}
.

For any natural number
N
, let
[N]:=\{0,\dots,N-1\}
. Given
p\leq p^{\prime}
and
q\leq q^{\prime}
, and
finite matrices
s\in(2^{[p+1]})^{[q+1]}

s^{\prime}\in(2^{[p^{\prime}+1]})^{[q^{\prime}+1]}
, let
s\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}
if for all
i\leq p
and
j\leq q

s(j)(i)=s^{\prime}(j)(i)
.
We will often indicate as
p_{s}
and
q_{s}
the (unique) natural numbers such that
s\in(2^{[p+1]})^{[q+1]}
.

Moreover, for all
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{X}
, let
s\sqsubseteq\omega
hold if for all
i\leq p_{s}
and
j\leq q_{s}
,
s(j)(i)=\omega(a_{j})(i)
.

Observe that if
s\sqsubseteq\omega
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)
.

For all
p,q
and
s\in(2^{[p+1]})^{[q+1]}
, we define a set of formulas
\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}\subseteq{F}_{p,q}
such that for all
p\leq p^{\prime}
and
q\leq q^{\prime}
,
s\in(2^{[p+1]})^{[q+1]}
and
s^{\prime}\in(2^{[p^{\prime}+1]})^{[q^{\prime}+1]}
,

s\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}
implies
\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}\subseteq\Gamma^{\langle p^{\prime},q^{\prime},s^{\prime}\rangle}
.

We let
\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}:=\bigcup_{n}\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{n}
, where the sets

\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{n}
are defined by a triple induction on
p,q
and
n
as follows:

\bullet

if
p=q=0
and
b=\omega_{0}(0)
,

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle 0,0,b\rangle}_{0}:=\Gamma\cup\{\lnot^{(1-b)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{0}^{0}\}
;

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle 0,0,b\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma_{n}\cup\{C^{0,0}_{n}\}
, if this is
A
-consistent, and

\Gamma^{\langle 0,0,b\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma_{n}^{\langle 0,0,b\rangle}
otherwise.

If
b\neq\omega_{0}(0)
, the definition is the same, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
in place of
A
;

\bullet

if
p>0
and
q=0
,
\Gamma^{\langle p,0,s\rangle}=\bigcup_{n}\Gamma^{\langle p,0,s\rangle}_{n}
, where if

s\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
,

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle p,0,s\rangle}_{0}:=\Gamma^{\langle p-1,0,s|_{p-1,0}\rangle}\cup\{\lnot^{(1-s(p)(0))}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{p}^{0}\}
;

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle p,0,s\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma^{\langle p,0,s\rangle}_{n}\cup\{C^{p,0}_{n}\}
if this is
A
-consistent, and

\Gamma^{\langle p,0,s\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma^{\langle p,0,s\rangle}_{n}
otherwise;

and if
s\not\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
, the definition is similar, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
in place of
A
;

\bullet

if
p=0
and
q>0
,
\Gamma^{\langle 0,q,s\rangle}=\bigcup_{n}\Gamma^{\langle 0,q,s\rangle}_{n}
, where if

s\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
,

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle 0,q,s\rangle}_{0}:=\Gamma^{\langle 0,q-1,s|_{0,q-1}\rangle}\cup\{\lnot^{(1-s(0)(q))}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{0}^{q}\}
;

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle 0,q,s\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma^{\langle 0,q,s\rangle}_{n}\cup\{C^{0,q}_{n}\}
if this is
A
-consistent, and

\Gamma^{\langle 0,q,s\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma^{\langle 0,q,s\rangle}_{n}
otherwise;

and if
s\not\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
, the definition is similar, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
in place of
A
;

\bullet

if both
p>0
and
q>0
,
\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}=\bigcup_{n}\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{n}
, where if

s\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
,

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{0}:=\Gamma^{\langle p-1,q,s|_{p-1,q}\rangle}\cup\Gamma^{\langle p,q-1,s|_{p,q-1}\rangle}\cup\{\lnot^{(1-s(p)(q))}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{p}^{q}\}
;

\bullet

\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{n}\cup\{C^{p,q}_{n}\}
if this is
A
-consistent, and

\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{n+1}:=\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}_{n}
otherwise;

and if
s\not\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
, the definition is similar, with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
in place of
A
.

In the following, whenever this creates no confusion, we will indicate
\Gamma^{\langle p,q,s\rangle}
simply as
\Gamma^{s}
.

For all finite matrices
s,s^{\prime}
, the following hold:

a.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\subseteq\Gamma^{s}
;

b.

if
s\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
,
\Gamma^{s}
is
A
-consistent;

c.

if
s\not\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
,
\Gamma^{s}
is consistent;

d.

if
s\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}
,
\Gamma^{s}\subseteq\Gamma^{s^{\prime}}
;

e.

if
\Gamma^{s}\vdash B
, then for all
t\sqsupseteq s
such that
B\in{F}_{p_{t},q_{t}}
,
B\in\Gamma^{t}
.

Facts a.-d. are verified by construction, so we only prove e.: suppose there exist
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\in\Gamma^{s}
such that
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}}B
and let
t\sqsupseteq s
be such that
B\in{F}_{p_{t},q_{t}}
. Suppose
B\notin\Gamma^{t}
: then for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Gamma^{t}_{k}\cup\{B\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
; yet, from d. it follows that
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\in\Gamma^{t}
, and thus
\Gamma^{t}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
, contradicting c.

Given matrices
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\sqsubseteq\omega
, let
\bigvee^{\omega}\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}
indicate the smallest sub-matrix of
\omega
extending all
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
(i.e. the restriction of
\omega
to
p=\max\{p_{s_{1}},\dots,p_{s_{n}}\}
and
q=\max\{q_{s_{1}},\dots,q_{s_{n}}\}
.

For any matrix
s
, let
\Gamma^{{\dagger}s}=\{A^{s}\mid A\in\Gamma^{s}\}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\to A\mid A\in\Gamma^{s}\}
.
Let
\Gamma^{{\dagger}}=\bigcup_{s}\Gamma^{{\dagger}s}
and let
\Gamma^{*}
be the deductive closure of
\Gamma^{{\dagger}}
.
We will establish the following properties of
\Gamma^{*}
:

\alpha.

\Gamma^{*}
is super-consistent;

\beta.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})\cup\Gamma^{*}
is
A
-consistent;

\gamma.

\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{*}
;

\delta.

if
B^{\omega}\notin\Gamma^{*}
and
B^{\omega}=B^{s}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{*}\cup\{B\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
;
moreover, if
B^{\omega_{0}}\notin\Gamma^{*}
and
B^{\omega_{0}}=B^{s}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{*}\cup\{B\}\vdash A
;

\epsilon.

\Gamma^{*}
is
\vee
-closed;

\eta.

\Gamma^{*}
is
\mathbf{C}
-closed.

This will conclude the proof of the theorem.

Let us preliminarily observe that any formula
B^{\omega}
can be written as
B^{s}
for a unique
s\sqsubseteq\omega
such that
p_{s}=|B|
and
q_{s}
is minimum with the property that
\mathsf{FN}(B)\subseteq\{a_{0},\dots,a_{q_{s}-1}\}
.

\alpha.

Let us show that
\Gamma^{{\dagger}}
is super-consistent. This immediately implies that
\Gamma^{*}
is super-consistent too.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma^{{\dagger}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
; then there exist
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}
and
B_{1}\in\Gamma^{s_{1}},\dots,B_{n}\in\Gamma^{s_{n}}
, such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega),B_{1}^{s_{1}},\dots,B_{n}^{s_{n}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
; we can suppose w.l.o.g. that
s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\sqsubseteq\omega
, since if
s_{i}\not\sqsubseteq\omega
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\vdash\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s_{i})
, and thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\vdash B_{i}^{s_{i}}
.

We have then
B_{i}^{s_{i}}=B_{i}^{\omega}
;
by letting
s=\bigvee^{\omega}\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n}\}
we thus have

B_{i}^{s_{i}}=B_{i}^{s}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s),B_{1}^{s},\dots,B_{n}^{s}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
,
which implies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s),B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
,
and since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\{B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\}\subseteq\Gamma^{s}
, we deduce

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}\in\Gamma^{s}
, contradicting a.

\beta.

The argument is similar to the one for
\alpha
.

\gamma.

Let
B\in\Gamma
and
s
be such that
B^{\omega}=B^{s}
,
p_{s}=|B|
and
\mathsf{FN}(B)\subseteq\{a_{0},\dots,a_{q_{s}-1}\}
.
For any
s^{\prime}\in(2^{[p_{s}+1]})^{[q_{s}+1]}
,
B^{s^{\prime}}\in\Gamma^{{\dagger}s^{\prime}}\subseteq\Gamma^{{\dagger}}
. Hence, using the fact that

\vdash\bigvee_{s\in(2^{[p_{s}+1]})^{[q_{s}+1]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)
, we deduce that

\{B^{s}\mid s\in(2^{[p_{s}+1]})^{[q_{s}+1]}\}\vdash B
, and thus that
\Gamma^{{\dagger}}\vdash B
, which implies
B\in\Gamma^{*}
.

\delta.

Let
s
be such that
B^{\omega}=B^{s}
. We consider the case of
\omega=\omega_{0}
; the case
\omega\neq\omega_{0}
is proved similarly with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
in place of
A
.
We will prove the contrapositive, i.e. that if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{{\dagger}}\cup\{B\}
is
A
-consistent, then
B^{\omega_{0}}=B^{s}\in\Gamma^{*}
, from which
\delta
. follows. Suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma^{{\dagger}}\cup\{B\}
is
A
-consistent. Suppose
B\notin\Gamma^{s}
: this implies
B\notin\Gamma
and that for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\Gamma^{s}_{k}\cup\{B\}\vdash A
. But this forces then
\Gamma^{s}\cup\{B\}\vdash A
;
since for all
F\in\Gamma^{s}
,
F^{s}\in\Gamma^{{\dagger}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s),F^{s}\vdash F
, we deduce then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{{\dagger}}\cup\{B\}\vdash A
, which is absurd. We conclude then that
B\in\Gamma^{s}
, and thus
B^{s}=B^{\omega}\in\Gamma^{{\dagger}}\subseteq\Gamma^{*}
.

\epsilon.

Again, we consider the case of
\omega=\omega_{0}
; the case
\omega\neq\omega_{0}
is proved similarly with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
in place of
A
.
Let
s\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
be such that
(B\lor C)^{\omega}=(B\lor C)^{s}
and suppose
(B\lor C)^{s}\in\Gamma^{*}
but neither
B^{s}\in\Gamma^{*}
nor
C^{s}\in\Gamma^{*}
; then by
\delta.
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{*}\cup\{B\}\vdash A
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{*}\cup\{C\}\vdash A
, hence

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{*}\cup\{B\lor C\}\vdash A
;
since
(B\lor C)^{s}\in\Gamma^{*}
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{*}\vdash B\lor C
, so we deduce

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\cup\Gamma^{*}\vdash A
, and since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s)\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})\cup\Gamma^{*}\vdash A
, contradicting
\beta
.

\eta.

Once more, we consider the case of
\omega=\omega_{0}
; the case
\omega\neq\omega_{0}
is proved similarly with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
in place of
A
.

Let
s\sqsubseteq\omega_{0}
be such that
(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B)^{\omega_{0}}=(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B)^{s}
and suppose
(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B)^{s}\in\Gamma^{*}
.

We can suppose w.l.o.g. that
a=a_{q}
with
q>q_{s}
.
Hence we can suppose that the formula
B^{\omega_{0}}
is of the form
B^{s+s^{\prime}+s^{\prime\prime}}
, for some finite matrices
s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime}
, with
s^{\prime\prime}\in 2^{[|B|+1]}
.

By Lemma
A.17
and Remark
2
we have that
\Gamma^{s}\vdash B\leftrightarrow(\bigvee_{v\in 2^{[|B|+1]}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land B_{s+v})
, where
B_{s+v}
has no free name.
Let
S=\{v\in 2^{[|B|+1]}\mid B_{s+v}\in\Gamma^{s}\}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}=\bigvee_{v\in S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)
.
Observe that for any
v\in S
,
\Gamma^{s+s^{\prime}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\to B
.
Let
v\in S
and
\omega_{v}
be such that
v\subseteq\omega_{v}
. Then

\Gamma^{s+s^{\prime}}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{v})\to B
.
Using e. we have then that for all
t\in 2^{[|B|+1]}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{v})\to B\in\Gamma^{s+s^{\prime}+t}
, so in particular,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{v})\to B\in\Gamma^{s+s^{\prime}+s^{\prime\prime}}
, so

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{v})\to B)^{\omega_{0}}\in\Gamma^{{\dagger}}\subseteq\Gamma^{*}
.
We thus have that for all
v\in S
,
\Gamma^{*}\vdash({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\to B)^{\omega_{0}}
, which implies

\Gamma^{*}\vdash({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\to B)^{\omega_{0}}
and thus
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\to B)^{\omega_{0}}\in\Gamma^{*}
.

At this point, if
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q
we are done.
Otherwise, suppose
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})<q
; if
v\notin S
, then
B_{s+v}\notin\Gamma^{s}
, which implies that for some
k
,

\Gamma^{s}_{k}\cup\{B_{s+v}\}\vdash A
; this implies that
\Gamma^{s}\vdash({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land B_{s+v})\to A
; we deduce then that
\Gamma^{s}\vdash B\to((\bigvee_{v\in S}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(v)\land B_{s+v})\lor A)
, and in particular that
\Gamma^{s}\vdash B\to({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\lor A)
;
this means that there exist formulas
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\in\Gamma^{s}\subseteq{F}_{p_{s},q_{s}}
, hence containing no occurrences of the name
a
, such that
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\vdash B\to({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\lor A)
;
from this we deduce first
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s),B_{1}^{s},\dots,B_{n}^{s}\vdash B\to({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\lor A)
, and then, using (
\mathbf{C}
E
2
),
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(s),\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B,B^{s}_{1},\dots,B^{s}_{n}\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\lor A)
. Now, since
s\subseteq\omega_{0}
,
\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}A,B_{1}^{s},\dots,B_{n}^{s}\in\Gamma^{*}
and

\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\lor A)\vdash(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\lor A
(Axiom (

C
∨

), since we can suppose w.l.o.g. that
a\notin\mathsf{FN}(A)
), we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})\cup\Gamma^{*}\vdash(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\lor A
; moreover, from
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})<q
we get (by Axiom (

C
⊥

))

\vdash\lnot\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}
and thus
(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\lor A\vdash A
, so we can conclude

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})\cup\Gamma^{*}\vdash A
, which contradicts
\beta
.

∎

Lemma A.21.

Let
\Gamma
be an
A
-consistent set of formulas. Then there exists a super-consistent set
\Delta
and
\omega\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Delta}
is
A
-consistent and its closure contains
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let us define sets of formulae
\Gamma_{a,i}
together with
\omega(\langle a,i\rangle)
as follows:

\bullet

let
\omega(\langle 0,0\rangle)=1
if
\Gamma\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{0}^{0}\}
is
A
-consistent, and
\omega(\langle 0,0\rangle)=0
otherwise;
moreover, let

\Gamma_{0,0}=\Gamma
;

\bullet

let
\omega(\langle a,i+1\rangle)=1
if
\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i+1}\}
is
A
-consistent, and
\omega(\langle a,i+1\rangle)=0
otherwise;
moreover, let

\Gamma_{a,i+1}=\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{\lnot^{(1-\omega(\langle a,i+1\rangle))}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i+1}\}
;

\bullet

let
\omega(\langle a+1,0\rangle)=1
if
\bigcup_{i}\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a+1}^{0}\}
is
A
-consistent, and
\omega(\langle a,i+1\rangle)=0
otherwise;
moreover, let

\Gamma_{a+1,0}=\bigcup_{i}\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{(\lnot^{1-\omega(\langle a+1,0\rangle))}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a+1}^{0}\}
.

By construction we then have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma=\bigcup_{a,i}\Gamma_{a,i}
;
let us show that

\bigcup_{a,i}\Gamma_{a,i}
is
A
-consistent:

\bullet

\Gamma_{0,0}=\Gamma
is
A
-consistent by hypothesis;

\bullet

suppose
\Gamma_{a,i}
is
A
-consistent; if
\Gamma_{a,i+1}=\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i+1}\}
then by construction
\Gamma_{a,i+1}
is
A
-consistent;
if
\Gamma_{a,i+1}=\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i+1}\}
then by construction
\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\}\vdash A
; if moreover

\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\}\vdash A
, the

\Gamma_{a,i}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\lor\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\}\vdash A
and thus

\Gamma_{a,i}\vdash A
, which is absurd.
We conclude then that
\Gamma_{a,i+1}
is
A
-consistent.

\bullet

suppose
\Gamma_{a,i}
is
A
-consistent for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
; first observe that
\Gamma_{a}:=\bigcup_{i}\Gamma_{a,i}
is
A
-consistent: if
\Gamma_{a}\vdash A
, then there exist
B_{1}\in\Gamma_{a,i_{1}},\dots,B_{n}\in\Gamma_{a,i_{n}}
such that
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\vdash A
; since for
j\leq k
,
\Gamma_{a,j}\subseteq\Gamma_{a,k}
, we deduce that
\Gamma_{a,\max\{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\}}\vdash A
, which is absurd.

Now, if
\Gamma_{a+1,0}=\Gamma_{a}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a+1}^{0}\}
then by construction
\Gamma_{a+1,0}
is
A
-consistent; if
\Gamma_{a+1,0}=\Gamma_{a}\cup\{\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a+1}^{0}\}
then

\Gamma_{a}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a+1}^{0}\}\vdash A
; hence, if
\Gamma_{a+1,0}\vdash A
, we deduce

\Gamma_{a}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a+1}^{0}\lor\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a+1}^{0}\}\vdash A
, so we conclude

\Gamma_{a}\vdash A
, which is absurd.

Now, for each formula
B\in\Gamma
let us define a formula
B^{*}
as follows:

B^{*}:=B\lor\left(\bigvee_{a\in\mathsf{FN}(B),i\leq|B|}\lnot^{\omega(\langle a,i\rangle)}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\right)

Observe that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\{B^{*}\}\vdash B
and that for all
\omega^{\prime}\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega^{\prime})\cup\{B^{*}\}
is consistent.

Let then
\Delta=\{B^{*}\mid B\in\Gamma\}
. It is clear that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Delta\vdash B
for all
B\in\Gamma
.
Suppose
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Delta\vdash A
, then there exists Boolean formulas
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}_{k}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)
and formulas
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\in\Gamma
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}_{k},B_{1}^{*},\dots,B_{n}^{*}\vdash A
. Since
B_{i}\vdash B^{*}
, this implies then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}_{1},\dots,{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}e}_{k},B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\vdash A
, and thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma
is not
A
-consistent, which is absurd.
∎

We now have all elements to proceed to the proof of the completeness theorem.

Theorem A.22 (completeness).

If
\Gamma\vDash A
, then
\Gamma\vdash_{\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}}A
.

Proof.

Let
{T}=\langle\Theta,\subseteq,\mathbf{i}\rangle
, where

\mathbf{i}({p})=\{\Gamma\mid{p}\in\Gamma\}
.

We will prove that for all
\Gamma\in\Theta
and
\omega\in\mathbf{B}_{X}
,

\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
iff
A^{\omega}\in\Gamma
.
From this claim the theorem is proved as follows: suppose
\Gamma\not\vdash A
; by Lemma
A.21
we obtain a super-consistent set
\Delta
and
\omega_{0}\in(2^{\mathbb{N}})^{{A}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})\cup\Delta
is
A
-consistent and its closure contains
\Gamma
.
By Lemma
A.20

\Delta
extends to
\Gamma^{*}\in\Theta
such that
\Gamma^{*}\supseteq\Gamma
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega_{0})\cup\Gamma^{*}
is
A
-consistent.
Then using the claim, from
\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{*}
, we deduce that
\Gamma^{*},\omega_{0}\vdash_{{T}}^{X}\Gamma
, and from
A\notin\Gamma^{*}
we deduce that

\Gamma^{*},\omega_{0}\not\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
; we can conclude then that
\Gamma\not\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
, and thus that
\Gamma\not\vDash A
.

Let us now prove the claim, by arguing by induction on
A
:

\bullet

if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
, from the vacuous assumption
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
we can freely deduce
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}^{\omega}\in\Gamma
; conversely,
from the vacuous assumption

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}^{\omega}\in\Gamma
we can freely deduce

\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
;

\bullet

if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}
, since
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}
always holds and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\top}^{\omega}\in\Gamma
always holds (since
\Gamma
is closed), we can conclude;

\bullet

if
A={p}
,
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}{p}
iff
{p}\in\Gamma
holds by definition (since
{p}\notin{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)
);

\bullet

if
A={\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}
, if
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
holds then
\omega(a)(i)=1
, hence
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}
, and thus
A^{\omega}
is logically valid, which implies
A^{\omega}\in\Gamma
by closure;
conversely, if
A^{\omega}\in\Gamma
, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma
is consistent, the only possibility is that
\omega(a)(i)=1
, which forces
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
.

\bullet

if
A=B\land C
, then
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
iff

\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}B
and
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}C
, which by the I.H. is equivalent to

B^{\omega}\in\Gamma
and
C^{\omega}\in\Gamma
, which is in turn equivalent to
(B\land C)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
.

\bullet

if
A=B\lor C
, and
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
, then either

\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}B
or
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}C
holds;
if the first holds, then by the I.H.
B^{\omega}\in\Gamma
, which implies
(B\lor C)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
, and one can argue similarly if the second holds;
conversely, if
(B\lor C)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
, by
\vee
-closure either
B^{\omega}\in\Gamma
or
C^{\omega}\in\Gamma
, so by the I.H. in each case we deduce
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
.

\bullet

if
A=B\to C
, then suppose that
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
; then for all
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\Theta
such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\supseteq\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}B
, also
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}C
holds.

Suppose
\Gamma\cup\{B^{\omega}\}
is super-consistent.

Furthermore, suppose

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma\cup\{B^{\omega}\}\not\vdash C^{\omega}
; then, by Lemma
A.20
(with
\omega_{0}=\omega
and
A=C
) there exists
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\Theta
with
\Gamma^{\prime}\supseteq\Gamma\cup\{B^{\omega}\}
such that
C^{\omega}\notin\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Using the I.H. we deduce then that
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}B
but
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega\not\vdash_{{T}}^{X}C
, against the assumption. We conclude then that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma\cup\{B^{\omega}\}\vdash C^{\omega}
, and thus that

\Gamma\vdash(B\to C)^{\omega}
, which implies by closure
(B\to C)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
.

Suppose now that
\Gamma\cup\{B^{\omega}\}
is not super-consistent. There exists
\omega^{\prime}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega^{\prime})\cup\Gamma\cup\{B^{\omega}\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega^{\prime})\cup\Gamma
is consistent the only possibility is that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega^{\prime})\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{|B|}_{\mathsf{FN}(B)}(\omega)
(which forces
\omega|_{|B|,\mathsf{FN}(B)}
to coincide with
\omega^{\prime}|_{|B|,\mathsf{FN}(B)}
) and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega^{\prime})\cup\Gamma\cup\{B\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
;
but then we also have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma\cup\{B\}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
, which implies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}(\omega)\cup\Gamma\vdash B\to C
and thus
\Gamma\vdash(B\to C)^{\omega}
; by closure
this implies then
(B\to C)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
.

Conversely, suppose
(B\to C)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
and let
\Gamma^{\prime}\in\Theta
be such that
\Gamma^{\prime}\supseteq\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}B
; by the I.H. this implies
B^{\omega}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
, and since

(B\to C)^{\omega}\in\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
we also deduce by closure

C^{\omega}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
, which by the I.H. implies
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}C
.

\bullet

if
A=\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B
, then suppose that
\Gamma,\omega\vdash_{{T}}^{X}A
; then by the I.H. the Borel set

S=\Big\{\omega^{\prime}\mid B^{\omega+\omega^{\prime}}\in\Gamma\Big\}\supseteq\Big\{\omega^{\prime}\mid\Gamma,\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{T}}B\Big\}

has measure greater than
q
. Observe that for all
\omega^{\prime}\in 2^{\mathbb{N}}
,

B^{\omega+\omega^{\prime}}
is equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{|B|_{X}}_{X}(\omega|_{X})\to\Big({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{|B|_{\{a\}}}_{\{a\}}(\omega^{\prime})\to B\Big)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}:=\bigvee\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\mathsf{Th}}^{|B|_{\{a\}}}_{\{a\}}(\omega^{\prime})\mid\omega^{\prime}\in S\}
; since
\Gamma
is closed, we deduce then that

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\to B)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
and that
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q
; again, by closure, this implies
(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
.

For the converse direction, suppose
(\mathbf{C}^{q}_{a}B)^{\omega}\in\Gamma
and let
\Gamma^{\prime}\supseteq\Gamma
; since
\Gamma
is
\mathbf{C}
-closed,
there exists a Boolean formula
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}
with
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q
such that
({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\to B)^{\omega}\in\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
. This implies that for all
\omega^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\rrbracket
,
B^{\omega+\omega^{\prime}}\in\Gamma^{\prime}
;
hence, by the I.H. for all
\omega^{\prime}\in\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\rrbracket
,
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{T}}B
.
Since
\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})\geq q
, the set of
\omega^{\prime}
such that
\Gamma^{\prime},\omega+\omega^{\prime}\Vdash^{X\cup\{a\}}_{{T}}B
has measure greater than
q
, and we can conclude
\Gamma,\omega\Vdash^{X}_{{T}}A
.

∎

A.5.
Permutative Rules in
\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}
.

We complete the picture of the normalization rules of
\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}
by considering permutative rules for
(\mathsf{m}
), illustrated in Fig.
11
, where we let
D_{a}^{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c})
be an abbreviation for

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b})\lor(\lnot{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a}^{i}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c})
.

It is easily checked that if
\Pi\rightarrowtriangle\Sigma
,
t^{\Pi}\rightarrowtriangle_{\{\}}t^{\Sigma}
, since the rules closely correspond to the permutation rules for
\oplus
in
\Lambda^{\{\}}_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
.

\mathsf{m}
)

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail B

\Pi

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail(A\to B)

\Sigma

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c}\rightarrowtail A

\Theta

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d}\rightarrowtail A

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\vDash D_{a}^{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}d})

(
\mathsf{m}
)

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail A

(
\to
E)

\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail B

A.6.
A “CbN” Proof-System.

The CHC described in Section
5
relates the proof-system
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
with the type system
\mathbf{C}\lambda^{\{\}}_{\to}
.
In this subsection we describe a “CbN” variant
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
of the proof-system
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
, for which it is possible to describe a CHC with the type system
\mathbf{C}\lambda_{\to}
.

In the correspondence from Section
5
, CbV application
\{t\}u
plays a fundamental role, as it translates the elimination rule of the counting quantifier. To obtain a translation into
\Lambda_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
we thus need to restrict the rule (
\mathbf{C}
E).

A sequent of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
is of the form
\Phi;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail A
, where
\Phi
and
\Gamma
are two sets of formulas, and
\Phi
contains
at most
one formula.
The fundamental intuition is that the formula, if any, in
\Phi
, has to be used
linearly
in the proof.

The rules of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
are illustrated in Fig.
13
.
Observe that, contrarily to
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
(see Section
3
), the rules include a “multiplication rule” (
\mathbf{C}\times
) to passes from
\mathbf{C}^{q}\mathbf{C}^{s}A
to
\mathbf{C}^{qs}A
.

\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
proves
less
formulas than
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
. Indeed, the restricted (
\mathbf{C}
E)-rule allows to deduce

\mathbf{C}^{q}B
from
\mathbf{C}^{q}A
only when
B
can be deduced
linearly
from
A
. For example, one cannot reproduce in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
the proof of
\mathbf{C}^{q}(A\to A)\to(A\to\mathbf{C}^{q}A)
illustrated in Section
3
, since the hypothesis

\mathbf{C}^{q}(A\to A)
, which is used as major premiss in a (
\mathbf{C}
E)-rule, should be used
twice
.
From the programming perspective, this means that one cannot encode in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
the non-linear “CbV” function

\lambda y.\lambda x.\{\lambda f.f(fx)\}y
.
For similar reasons, it seems that one cannot prove
\mathbf{C}^{q}A\to(A\to A\to B)\to\mathbf{C}^{q}B
in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
, while this can be proved in
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}}
as shown in Fig.
12
. From the programming perspective, this means that one cannot encode the
non-linear “CbV” function
\lambda x.\lambda y.\{\lambda y.yxx\}x
.

\mathbf{C}^{q}A,A\to A\to A\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}A

A,A\to A\to A\vdash A\to A\to B

A,A\to A\to A\vdash A

(
\to
E)

A,A\to A\to A\vdash A\to B

A,A\to A\to A\vdash A

(
\to
E)

A,A\to A\to A\vdash B

(
\mathbf{C}
E)

\mathbf{C}^{q}A,A\to A\to A\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}B

(
\to
I)

\vdash\mathbf{C}^{q}A\to(A\to A\to A)\to\mathbf{C}^{q}B

.

\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL}}
of
\mathbf{C}^{q}A\to(A\to A\to A)\to\mathbf{C}^{q}A
.

and exploits the admissibility of the following rule:

;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{s_{1}*\dots*s_{n}}A

∗
)

Figure 14
.
Normalization steps of
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
.

The translation from
\mathbf{ND}_{\mathsf{iCPL0}}^{\mathrm{CbN}}
to
\mathbf{C}\lambda_{\to}
relies on properties of

head contexts
in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
. These are defined by the grammar below:

\mathtt{H}[\ ]::=[\ ]\mid\lambda x.\mathtt{H}[\ ]\mid\mathtt{H}[\ ]u

The fundamental property of head contexts is that they naturally behave as CbV functions, due to the following lemma:

Lemma A.23.

For any head context
\mathtt{H}[\ ]
and term
t
,

\mathtt{H}[\nu a.t]\ \rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}^{*}\ \nu a.\mathtt{H}[t]
.

Proof.

By induction on
\mathtt{H}[\ ]
:

\bullet

if
\mathtt{H}[\ ]=[\ ]
, the claim is immediate;

\bullet

if
\mathtt{H}[\ ]=\lambda x.\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[\ ]
, then we have

\mathtt{H}[\nu a.t]=\lambda x.\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[\nu a.t]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{IH}}}{{\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}^{*}}}\lambda x.\nu a.\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[t]\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}\nu a.\lambda x.\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[t]=\nu a.\mathtt{H}[t]
;

\bullet

if
\mathtt{H}[\ ]=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[\ ]u
, then we have

\mathtt{H}[\nu a.t]=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[\nu a.t]u\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{IH}}}{{\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}^{*}}}(\nu a.\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[t])u\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}\nu a.\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[t]u=\nu a.\mathtt{H}[t]
.

∎

In other words, whenever
t
is a function of the form
\lambda x.\mathtt{H}[x]
for some head context
\mathtt{H}[\ ]
, CbN and CbV application of
t
coincide, since

t(\nu a.v)
and
\nu a.tv
have the same normal form.

This property is reflected in the following:

Lemma A.24.

For any head context
\mathtt{H}[\ ]
, the following rule is derivable in
\mathbf{C}\lambda_{\to}
:

\Gamma\vdash t:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{qs}\sigma

x:\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma,\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{H}[x]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{r}\tau

(head-subst)

\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{H}[t]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{rs}\tau

Proof.

By induction on
\mathtt{H}[\ ]
:

\bullet

if
\mathtt{H}[\ ]=[\ ]
, then it must be
q=r
, and the claim is then immediate;

\bullet

if
\mathtt{H}[\ ]=\lambda y.\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[\ ]
, then
\tau=\mathfrak{t}\Rightarrow\tau^{\prime}
and we must have

x:\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma,\Gamma,y:\mathfrak{t}\vdash\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[x]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}\tau^{\prime}
. Then by IH we deduce

\Gamma,y:\mathfrak{t}\vdash\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[t]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{rs}\tau^{\prime}
, and finally

\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{H}[t]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{rs}\tau
.

\bullet

if
\mathtt{H}[\ ]=\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[\ ]u
, then we must have

x:\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma,\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[x]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{q}(\mathfrak{t}\Rightarrow\tau)
and

x:\mathbf{C}^{q}\sigma,\Gamma\vdash u:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathfrak{t}
. Then by IH we deduce

\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{H}^{\prime}[t]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{rs}(\mathfrak{t}\Rightarrow\tau)
, and we can thus conclude

\Gamma\vdash\mathtt{H}[t]:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathbf{C}^{rs}\tau
.

∎

To any formula
A
of
\mathsf{iCPL}_{0}
, we associate a non-quantified type
\sigma_{A}
and a positive real
|A|\in(0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
of
\mathbf{C}\lambda_{\to}
as follows:

\displaystyle\sigma_{{p}}

\displaystyle:=o

\displaystyle|o|:=1

\displaystyle\sigma_{A\to B}

\displaystyle:=(\mathbf{C}^{|A|}\sigma_{A})\Rightarrow\sigma_{B}

\displaystyle|A\to B|=|B|

\displaystyle\sigma_{\mathbf{C}^{q}A}

\displaystyle:=\sigma_{A}

\displaystyle|\mathbf{C}^{q}A|=q\cdot|A|

We let then
\mathfrak{s}_{A}:=\mathbf{C}^{|A|}\sigma_{A}
(observe that
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{C}^{q}A}=\mathbf{C}^{q\cdot|A|}\sigma_{A}
).

The translation of a derivation
\Pi
of
\Phi;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail A
into a typing derivation

D^{\Pi}
of
\mathfrak{s}_{\Phi},\mathfrak{s}_{\Gamma}\vdash t^{\Pi}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathfrak{s}_{A}
in
\mathbf{C}\lambda_{\to}
is illustrated in Fig.
15
, where we exploit the fact that if
\Phi=\{A\}
is non-empty,
then
t^{\Pi}=t^{\Pi}[x:\mathfrak{s}_{A}]
is a head context (as it can be checked by induction on the construction).
We omit the case of the rule (
\mathbf{C}_{\times}
), as it follows immediately from the induction hypothesis, since
\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{C}^{q}\mathbf{C}^{s}A}=\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbf{C}^{qs}A}
.

The stability of the translation under normalization is easily checked using Lemma
A.23
.

(id)

;\Gamma,A\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail A

\mapsto

(id)

\mathfrak{s}_{\Gamma},y:\mathfrak{s}_{A}\vdash y:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathfrak{s}_{A}

(id
lin
)

A;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail A

\mapsto

(id)

x:\mathfrak{s}_{A},\mathfrak{s}_{\Gamma}\vdash x:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathfrak{s}_{A}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}

(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}
)

\ ;\Gamma\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}\rightarrowtail A

\mapsto

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\vDash{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\bot}

(
\vee
)

\mathfrak{s}_{\Gamma}\vdash\mathbf{c}:{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}\rightarrowtail\mathfrak{s}_{A}

\Pi

Appendix B
Details about the Probabilistic Event
\lambda
-Calculus.

B.1.
The Language
\Lambda_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
.

Let us first comment on how Theorem
4.1
(i.e. confluence and strong normalization of
\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}
, and confluence of
\rightarrowtriangle
) follows from
confluence of
\rightarrowtriangle
in the calculus from
[
16
]
.
The
\lambda
-calculus in
[
16
]
slightly differs from our presentation of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
, since choice operators do not depend on indexes
i\in\mathbb{N}
. However, if
\varphi
is any bijection from
\mathbb{N}^{2}
to
\mathbb{N}
,
one can define an invertible embedding
t\mapsto t^{\varphi}
from
\Lambda_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
to the calculus in
[
16
]
by replacing

t\oplus^{i}_{a}u
with
t^{\varphi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi(a,i)}}{{\oplus}}u^{\varphi}
and

(\nu a.t)^{\varphi}=\nu\varphi(a,0).\dots.\nu\varphi(a,\mathsf{ord}_{a}(t)).t^{\varphi}
, where
\mathsf{ord}_{a}(t)
is the
maximum
i
s.t.
\oplus_{a}^{i}
occurs in
t
.
Since the permutative rules in
[
16
]
translate into those in Fig.
4
under this translation, the results from
[
16
]
can be transported to our language.

We now discuss PNFs in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
.

Definition B.1.

Let
S
be a set of name-closed
\lambda
-terms and
a
be a name.
For all
i\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{-1\}
, the set of
(S,a)
-trees of level
i
is defined as follows:

\bullet

any
t\in S
is a
(S,a)
-tree of level
-1
;

\bullet

if
t,u
are
(S,a)
-trees of level
j
and
k
, respectively, and
j,k<i
, then
t\oplus_{a}^{i}u
is a
(S,a)
-tree of level
i
.

The
support
of a
(S,a)
-tree
t
, indicated as
\mathrm{Supp}(t)
, is the finite set of terms in
S
which are leaves of
t
.

Definition B.2.

The sets
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{V}
of name-closed terms are defined inductively as follows:

\bullet

all variable
x\in\mathcal{V}
;

\bullet

if
t\in\mathcal{V}
,
\lambda x.t\in\mathcal{V}
;

\bullet

if
t\in\mathcal{V}
and
u\in\mathcal{T}
, then
tu\in\mathcal{V}
;

\bullet

if
t\in\mathcal{V}
, then
t\in\mathcal{T}
;

\bullet

if
t
is a
(\mathcal{T},a)
-tree, then
\nu a.t\in\mathcal{T}
.

Lemma B.3.

For all name-closed term
t\in\mathcal{T}
,
t\in\mathcal{V}
iff it does not start with
\nu
.

Proof.

First observe that if
t=\nu a.b
, then
t\notin\mathcal{V}
. For the converse direction, we argue by induction on
t\neq\nu a.b
:

\bullet

if
t=x
then
t\in\mathcal{V}
;

\bullet

if
t=\lambda x.u
then by IH
u\in\mathcal{V}
, so
t\in\mathcal{V}
;

\bullet

if
t=uv
, then the only possibility is that
t\in\mathcal{V}
;

\bullet

if
t=u\oplus_{a}^{i}v
, then
t
is not name-closed, again the hypothesis.

∎

For all term
t
and
a\in\mathsf{FN}(t)
, we let
I_{a}(t)
be the maximum index
i
such that
\oplus_{a}^{i}
occurs in
t
, and
I_{a}(t)=-1
if
\oplus_{a}^{i}
does not occur in
t
for all index
i
.

We now show that Definition
B.2
precisely captures PNF.

Lemma B.4.

A name-closed term
t
is in PNF iff
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

Proof.

(
\Rightarrow
):

We argue by induction on
t
.
If
t
has no bound name, then
t
is obviously in
\mathcal{T}
. Otherwise:

\bullet

if
t=\lambda x.u
, then
u
is also name-closed. Hence, by IH
u\in\mathcal{T}
; observe that
u
cannot start with
\nu
(as
t
would not be normal) so by Lemma
B.3
,
u\in\mathcal{V}
, which implies
t\in\mathcal{T}
;

\bullet

if
t=uv
, then
u
and
v
are both name-closed, and so by induction
u,v\in\mathcal{T}
; if
u
started with
\nu
, then
t
would not be normal, hence, by Lemma
B.3
,
u\in\mathcal{V}
, and we conclude then that
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

\bullet

if
t=u\oplus_{a}^{i}v
, then it cannot be name-closed;

\bullet

if
t=\nu a.u
, then we show, by a sub-induction on
u
, that
u
is a
(\mathcal{T},a)
-tree of level
I_{a}(u)
: first note that
I_{a}(u)
cannot be
-1
, since otherwise
\nu a.u\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}u
, so
u
would not be normal. We now consider all possible cases for
u
:

\bullet

u
cannot be a variable, or
I_{a}(u)
would be
-1
;

\bullet

If
u=\lambda x.v
, then
I_{a}(v)=I_{a}(u)
and so by sub-IH
v
is a
(\mathcal{T},a)
-tree of level
I_{a}(u)
, which implies that
u=\lambda x.v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{I_{a}(u)}v_{2}
, which is not normal. Absurd.

\bullet

if
u=vw
, then let
J=I_{a}(u)=\max\{I_{a}(v),I_{a}(w)\}
cannot be
-1
, since otherwise
I_{a}(u)
would be
-1
. Hence
J\geq 0
is either
I_{a}(v)
or
I_{a}(w)
. We consider the two cases separatedly:

\bullet

if
J=I_{a}(v)
, then by sub-IH,
v=v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}v_{2}
, and thus

u=(v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}v_{2})w
is not normal;

\bullet

if
J=I_{a}(w)
, then by sub-IH,
w=w_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}w_{2}
, and thus

u=v(w_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}w_{2})
is not normal.

In any case we obtain an absurd conclusion.

\bullet

if
u=u_{1}\oplus_{a}^{i}u_{2}
, then if
u_{1},u_{2}
are both in
\mathcal{V}
, we are done, since
u
is a
(\mathcal{T},a)
-tree of level
i=I_{a}(u)
. Otherwise, if
i<I_{a}(u)
, then
J=I_{a}(u)=\max\{I_{a}(v),I_{a}(w)\}
, so we consider two cases:

\bullet

if
J=I_{a}(v)>i
, then by sub-IH,
v=v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}v_{2}
, and thus

u=(v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}v_{2})\oplus_{a}^{i}u_{2}
is not normal;

\bullet

if
J=I_{a}(w)>i
, then by sub-IH,
w=w_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}w_{2}
, and thus

u=v\oplus_{a}^{i}(w_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}w_{2})
is not normal.

We conclude then that
i=I_{a}(u)
; we must then show that
I_{a}(v),I_{a}(w)<i
. Suppose first
I_{a}(v)\geq i
, then
(v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{J}v_{2})\oplus_{a}^{i}u_{2}
is not normal. In a similar way we can show that

I_{a}(w)<i
.

\bullet

if
u=\nu b.v
, then
I_{a}(u)=I_{a}(v)
, so by sub-IH
v=v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{I_{a}(u)}v_{2}
, and we conclude that
u=\nu b.v_{1}\oplus_{a}^{i}v_{2}
is not normal.

(
\Leftarrow
):

It suffices to check by induction on
t\in\mathcal{T}
that it is in PNF.

∎

Corollary B.5.

A name-closed term of the form
\nu a.t
is in PNF iff
t
is a
(\mathcal{T},a)
-tree of level
I_{a}(t)\geq 0
.

Let us conclude by discussing
randomized trees
and
head-reduction
.
With any term
t
we associate a labeled finitely branching tree
RBT(t)
as follows:

\bullet

if
t\rightarrowtriangle^{*}_{\mathsf{h}}u
, where
u=\lambda x_{1}.\dots.\lambda x_{n}.yu_{1}\dots u_{n}
is a HNV, then
RBT(t)
only consists of one node labeled
u
;

\bullet

if
t\rightarrowtriangle^{*}_{\mathsf{h}}u
, where
u
is a PNF of the form
\nu a.T
,
T
is a
({T},a)
-tree and
\mathrm{supp}(T)=\langle u_{1},\dots,u_{n}\rangle
, then
RBT(t)
has root labeled
\nu a.T
and coincides with the syntactic tree of
T
, with leaves replaced by
RBT(u_{1}),\dots,RBT(u_{n})
;

\bullet

otherwise,
RBT(t)
has a root labeled
\Omega
and no sub-trees.

Proposition B.6.

If
t
reduces to
u
by either
\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}}
or head
\beta
-reduction, then
RBT(t)\sqsubseteq RBT(u)
.

A
randomized path in
RBT(t)
is any path
\pi
in
RBT(t)
starting from the root and ending in a leaf.
A randomized path in
RBT(t)
is thus of one of the following two forms:

(1)

a finite path

\pi=\langle\nu a_{1}.T_{1},\nu a_{2}.T_{2},\dots,\nu a_{N}.T_{N},\lambda x_{1}\dots x_{n}.y\rangle
, where

v\leadsto\nu a_{i+1}.T_{i+1}
holds for some
v\in\mathrm{supp}(T_{i})
;

(2)

an infinite path

\pi=\langle\nu a_{1}.T_{1},\nu a_{2}.T_{2},\dots,\nu a_{n}.T_{n},\dots\rangle
, where

v\leadsto\nu a_{i+1}.T_{i+1}
holds for some
v\in\mathrm{supp}(T_{i})
.

With any such path
\pi
we can associate an (either finite or infinite) list of words
b^{\pi}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, where
b^{\pi}_{i}
is the list of choices leading from
\nu a.T_{i}
to the unique term
v\in\mathrm{supp}(T_{i})
such that
v\leadsto\nu a_{i+1}.T_{i+1}
.

If
\pi
is a finite randomized path in
RBT(t)
ending in some
u\in{V}
, we say that
\pi
is a randomized path
from
t
to
u
(noted
\pi:t\mapsto u
).

Let us consider the (unique) Borel
\sigma
-algebra
\mu
on
2^{\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}}
satisfying
\mu(C_{i,j})=1/2
, where
C_{i,j}
is the cylinder
\{\omega\mid\omega(i,j)=1\}
.

Any randomized path
\pi
yields a Borel set
B_{\pi}:=\bigcap_{i,j}C_{i,(b^{\pi}_{i})_{j}}
, so that two distinct paths
\pi\neq\pi^{\prime}
are such that
B_{\pi}\cap B_{\pi^{\prime}}=\emptyset
.
Moreover, if
\pi
is finite, the Borel set
B_{\pi}
is captured by the Boolean formula

{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{\pi}:=\bigwedge_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{\pi}^{i}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{\pi}^{i}=\bigwedge_{j}(\lnot)^{1+(b^{\pi}_{i})_{j}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}x}_{a_{i}}^{j}
.

Lemma B.7.

For all terms
t\in{T}
and
u\in{V}
,

{D}_{t}(u)=\mu\left(\bigcup\{B_{\pi}\mid\pi:t\mapsto u\}\right)=\sum_{\pi:t\mapsto u}\mu({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}b}_{\pi})
.

Lemma B.8.

For all
t\in{T}
, the following are equivalent:

(i.)

t
has a head-normal form;

(iii.)

RBT(t)
is finite.

Proof.

(i.)
\Rightarrow
(iii.) is proved by induction on the length of a reduction of
t
to a head-normal form.
For
(iii.)
\Rightarrow
(i.), observe that
RBT(t)
contains an infinite path only if
t
admits an infinite head-reduction.
From (iii.)
we deduce then that all head reductions of
t
are finite, so
t
has a head-normal form.∎

B.2.
The Language
\Lambda^{\{\}}_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
.

In this section we study some properties of reduction
\rightarrowtriangle_{\{\}}
in the calculus
\Lambda^{\{\}}_{\mathsf{{PE}}}
. Most arguments closely resemble arguments from
[
16
]
, so we omit several details.

Lemma B.9.

\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}\{\}}
is strongly normalizing.

Proof.

We follow the proof of [
[
16
]
, Lemma 7], we define the partial order
\prec_{M}
by

\displaystyle\oplus_{a}^{i}

\displaystyle\prec_{M}\oplus_{b}^{j}

\displaystyle\text{ if }(a,i)<_{M}(b,j)

\displaystyle\oplus_{a}^{i}

\displaystyle\prec_{M}\nu b

\displaystyle\text{ for all labels }a,b

\displaystyle\nu a

\displaystyle\prec_{M}@,\lambda x

\displaystyle\text{ fo any label }b

\displaystyle@,\lambda x

\displaystyle\prec_{M}\{\}

One can check then that the well-founded recursive path ordering
<
defined by

\displaystyle t<u\ \Leftrightarrow\ \begin{cases}[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]<[u_{1},\dots,u_{m}]&\text{ if }f=g\\
[t_{1},\dots,t_{n}]<[u]&\text{ if }f\prec_{M}g\\
[t]\leq[u_{1},\dots,u_{m}]&\text{ if }f\not\prec_{B}g\end{cases}

where
t=f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
and
u=g(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
, with
f,g\in\{\oplus_{a}^{i},\nu a.,@,\lambda x,\{\}\}
, is such that
t\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}\{\}}u
implies
u<t
.
∎

Lemma B.10.

\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}\{\}}
is confluent.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.9
and Newman’s Lemma, it suffices to check local confluence. All rules from (

ν

) from Fig.

, as well as rules (

), can be written under the general form

(

)

\displaystyle\mathtt{C}[t\oplus_{a}^{i}u]\rightarrowtriangle_{\mathsf{p}\{\}}\mathtt{C}[t]\oplus_{a}^{i}\mathtt{C}[u]

where
\mathtt{C}[\ ]
is defined by the grammar

\mathtt{C}[\ ]::=[\ ]\mid\lambda x.\mathtt{C}[\ ]\mid\mathtt{C}[\ ]u\mid t\mathtt{C}[\ ]\mid\mathtt{C}[\ ]\oplus_{a}^{i}u\mid t\oplus_{a}^{i}\mathtt{C}[\ ]\mid\nu a.\mathtt{C}[\ ]\mid\{\mathtt{C}[\ ]\}u\mid\{t\}\mathtt{C}[\ ]

We consider all rules against each other. All cases involving (

⁣
⋆

‣
B.2
) can be treated as in the proof of [

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{t\}\nu a.u\oplus_{b}^{i}v}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu a.t(u\oplus_{b}^{i}v)}

∎

Theorem B.11.

\rightarrowtriangle_{\{\}}
is confluent.

Proof.

The argument closely follows the one from [
[
16
]
, pp. 9-12], using the observation that
none of the permutations (

⁢

{
}

ν

)-(

{
}

⊕
2

) can either block a
\beta
-redex or figure in a critical pair with a
\beta
-redex (in other words,
\beta
-reduction commutes with (

⁢

{
